Or at least I haven’t noticed it in that general way. I can get tense during a hectic school day, but that’s kind of normal, like when the copier jams right before class or someone spills glue everywhere.\n\nBut if you mean just sitting there feeling anxious for no reason, I don’t know… I wouldn’t say that’s a big thing for me. It’s more that something specific sets me off, and then I get stuck on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or repeating these actions helps to reduce your anxiety or unease about possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s exactly why I do it. Like, if I check the stove or the pill organizer again, or look back over my lesson plans one more time, there’s this little drop in the nervous feeling.\n\nIt’s not like I enjoy doing it. I’ll be standing there with my bag on my shoulder, keys in my hand, and then I’m thinking, “Did I actually do that, or did I just think about doing it?” And then I go back. Sometimes I don’t even touch anything, I just sort of replay it in my head until it feels… settled enough.\n\nBut then, of course, I’m running late, and then I’m annoyed with myself. My students always notice when I come in a little flustered, too, which is embarrassing. So it helps for a minute, I guess, but it also kind of causes problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, beyond just these worries about forgetting something or not doing something correctly?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s those kinds of thoughts, I think. Like the “did I forget something important” kind.\n\nIt’s not just, “Oh shoot, I forgot my lunch,” it feels more nagging than that—like I’ll suddenly think, did I leave my aunt’s medicine out wrong, or did I set up something in the classroom in a way that isn’t safe? And even if I’m pretty sure I handled it, the thought pops back in anyway.\n\nSorry, I’m thinking of my classroom because yesterday the kids were using scissors and glue sticks and one of them somehow glued a paper to his sleeve, which is not really the point. But yeah—the repeating thoughts are usually about whether I missed something important or didn’t do it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other thoughts or urges that keep coming back and feel like you need to act on them, aside from the worries about forgetting something or not doing something correctly?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there are a bunch of other ones, no. It’s mostly that same sort of… did I forget something important, did I leave something wrong.\n\nLike with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll think, “Wait, did I set that out correctly?” even when I’m pretty sure I did. Or at school, if I set up materials for the kids, I’ll suddenly wonder if I made it unsafe somehow. Which is silly sometimes because it’ll be, like, construction paper and glue sticks, but then you know, kids can turn anything into chaos.\n\nSo, not really other urges, I guess. It’s more that doubt that keeps coming back and makes me want to check or mentally go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts or behaviors are interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still doing everything I need to do. I get to work, I take care of family things, all of that. It’s not like I’m just not functioning.\n\nBut it does make things harder, especially in the mornings. I’ll think I’m ready to leave and then suddenly I’m checking something again, and then somehow ten minutes are gone. Then I get to school feeling like I’m already behind before the kids have even come in, which is not a great way to start a day.\n\nAnd lesson prep takes longer sometimes, or it feels less… smooth, I guess. I used to actually like sitting down with my journal in the evening too, just to unwind, and now even that doesn’t feel as calming as it used to. I’ll be trying to write and then my brain is still circling back to whether I did everything right. So, it’s not ruining my life, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors beyond checking things, like a specific way of doing daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Not really like… I don’t have a special way I have to tap things or count, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that before I leave the house, or before I leave my classroom, I end up going back over the same things a few times. Like I’ll check my aunt’s pill organizer, or the stove, or my lesson plans, and then sometimes I’ll just stand there silently replaying what I did in my head.\n\nAnd I know that sounds like checking, but it’s also this whole little routine of “okay, let me make sure one more time,” until I feel calm enough to actually walk away. Which is annoying because then I’m late, and then I’m annoyed at myself, and then the kids come in asking if we’re doing art today and I’m still mentally back at the stove.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts mostly about preventing something bad from happening or making a mistake, rather than other types of concerns?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly. It’s not really like random worries about… I don’t know, germs or something else. It’s more that nagging thought that I forgot something important or made a mistake that could matter.\n\nLike with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll think, “Did I leave it out wrong?” even if I know I probably handled it. Or with my classroom stuff, I’ll start wondering if I set something up in a way that isn’t safe, even when it’s just normal supplies and I’ve done it a million times.\n\nAnd then my brain just keeps bringing it back up. It’s kind of like when you leave the grocery store and you’re sure you forgot one thing, except it’s not about bananas, it’s about something that feels more serious. So yeah, I’d say it’s mostly about preventing something bad or not messing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are affecting your relationships with your students, colleagues, or family members?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really hurting my relationships exactly… like, I’m still showing up for my students, and I’m doing what I need to do with my family.\n\nBut I do notice I come in already kind of rushed sometimes, especially in the mornings. Like I’ll get to school and feel behind before the day has even started, and then I’m trying to switch into teacher mode while my brain is still catching up. Kids pick up on energy, you know? I always tell them, “Take a breath, start fresh,” which is funny because I’m not always doing that myself.\n\nWith colleagues, I don’t think anyone’s upset with me or anything. It’s more that lesson prep can take me longer, or it doesn’t feel as smooth. And at home, even my journaling — which used to be my little reset thing — doesn’t feel as restful as it used to. So, relationships… maybe not directly, but it affects the way I’m arriving to people, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel like these thoughts or behaviors are causing problems for you that we haven't talked about yet?\n\nPatient: I mean, not in a huge, life-falling-apart way. I’m still getting to work, still helping with my family stuff, still doing the things I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut mornings are probably the biggest one. They just take longer than they should, and then I get to school feeling like I’m already behind, which is the worst feeling when you’ve got a room full of kids coming in with all their stories and backpacks and someone can’t find their folder. And I’m trying to be calm, but in my head I’m like, “Okay, you were supposed to be here ten minutes earlier.”\n\nAnd lesson prep too, a little. It’s not that I can’t do it, it just doesn’t feel as smooth as it used to. Even my journaling at night — I used to really like that, I have all these half-filled notebooks, some with nice covers I bought because I thought that would make me more consistent — but lately it doesn’t feel as restful. It’s more like another thing I’m trying to settle my brain with, and it doesn’t always work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that feel like they are forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s actually why I came in. It’s like I’ll be trying to think about the day, or what I need for class, and then this thought just pops in like, “Wait, did you forget something important?”\n\nLike with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll suddenly wonder if I left it out wrong, even if I’m pretty sure I didn’t. Or I’ll be in my classroom and start thinking, “Did I set those materials up safely?” even though it’s normal stuff and I’ve done it before.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, it’s just… nagging. Like the doubt keeps coming back and interrupting whatever I was trying to focus on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts are coming into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say so. It’s not like I sit down and choose to think, “Let me worry about this again.” It just kind of barges in.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of something totally normal — planning a lesson, or honestly even making coffee, which I keep meaning to stop buying from that one place because it’s gotten so expensive — and then my brain goes, “What if you forgot something important?” Like my aunt’s medicine, or whether I set something up safely in the classroom.\n\nAnd even when I can tell myself, “No, you probably handled it,” the doubt still pops back up. So yes, it does feel against my will, I guess. Like nagging, more than anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts are unwelcome and you wish you could stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely unwelcome. I mean, I don’t want to be standing there thinking about whether I left my aunt’s medicine out wrong when I know I already dealt with it, or replaying whether the classroom materials are set up safely.\n\nIt’s like the thought pops in and I’m trying to move on, and then it’s back again. And I know that sounds small, but it’s just annoying and tiring. Like, I wish my brain would just accept, “You handled it,” and let me go on with my day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Or with my classroom stuff, I’ll start wondering if I set something up in a way that isn’t safe, even when it’s just normal supplies and I’ve done it a million times.\n\nAnd then my brain just keeps bringing it back up. It’s kind of like when you leave the grocery store and you’re sure you forgot one thing, except it’s not about bananas, it’s about something that feels more serious. So yeah, I’d say it’s mostly about preventing something bad or not messing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are affecting your relationships with your students, colleagues, or family members?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really hurting my relationships exactly… like, I’m still showing up for my students, and I’m doing what I need to do with my family.\n\nBut I do notice I come in already kind of rushed sometimes, especially in the mornings. Like I’ll get to school and feel behind before the day has even started, and then I’m trying to switch into teacher mode while my brain is still catching up. Kids pick up on energy, you know? I always tell them, “Take a breath, start fresh,” which is funny because I’m not always doing that myself.\n\nWith colleagues, I don’t think anyone’s upset with me or anything. It’s more that lesson prep can take me longer, or it doesn’t feel as smooth. And at home, even my journaling — which used to be my little reset thing — doesn’t feel as restful as it used to. So, relationships… maybe not directly, but it affects the way I’m arriving to people, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel like these thoughts or behaviors are causing problems for you that we haven't talked about yet?\n\nPatient: I mean, not in a huge, life-falling-apart way. I’m still getting to work, still helping with my family stuff, still doing the things I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut mornings are probably the biggest one. They just take longer than they should, and then I get to school feeling like I’m already behind, which is the worst feeling when you’ve got a room full of kids coming in with all their stories and backpacks and someone can’t find their folder. And I’m trying to be calm, but in my head I’m like, “Okay, you were supposed to be here ten minutes earlier.”\n\nAnd lesson prep too, a little. It’s not that I can’t do it, it just doesn’t feel as smooth as it used to. Even my journaling at night — I used to really like that, I have all these half-filled notebooks, some with nice covers I bought because I thought that would make me more consistent — but lately it doesn’t feel as restful. It’s more like another thing I’m trying to settle my brain with, and it doesn’t always work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that feel like they are forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s actually why I came in. It’s like I’ll be trying to think about the day, or what I need for class, and then this thought just pops in like, “Wait, did you forget something important?”\n\nLike with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll suddenly wonder if I left it out wrong, even if I’m pretty sure I didn’t. Or I’ll be in my classroom and start thinking, “Did I set those materials up safely?” even though it’s normal stuff and I’ve done it before.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, it’s just… nagging. Like the doubt keeps coming back and interrupting whatever I was trying to focus on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts are coming into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say so. It’s not like I sit down and choose to think, “Let me worry about this again.” It just kind of barges in.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of something totally normal — planning a lesson, or honestly even making coffee, which I keep meaning to stop buying from that one place because it’s gotten so expensive — and then my brain goes, “What if you forgot something important?” Like my aunt’s medicine, or whether I set something up safely in the classroom.\n\nAnd even when I can tell myself, “No, you probably handled it,” the doubt still pops back up. So yes, it does feel against my will, I guess. Like nagging, more than anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts are unwelcome and you wish you could stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely unwelcome. I mean, I don’t want to be standing there thinking about whether I left my aunt’s medicine out wrong when I know I already dealt with it, or replaying whether the classroom materials are set up safely.\n\nIt’s like the thought pops in and I’m trying to move on, and then it’s back again. And I know that sounds small, but it’s just annoying and tiring. Like, I wish my brain would just accept, “You handled it,” and let me go on with my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts are intrusive, meaning they interrupt your other thoughts or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, they do interrupt me. That’s kind of what made me finally come in, because I’ll be doing something else and then this thought cuts in, like, “Did you forget something important?”\n\nIt’ll be about my aunt’s medicine, like maybe I left it out wrong, or at school I’ll suddenly wonder if I didn’t set up the classroom materials safely. And it’s not like I really think I did something terrible, but the doubt just keeps popping back into my head anyway.\n\nLike I’ll try to get back to what I was doing, and then—sorry, this reminds me, I even do this when I’m trying to get ready in the morning and I’m already running late, which is always when my coffee maker takes forever—but yeah, the thought comes back and kind of pulls me away from whatever I was focused on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… somewhat, but I wouldn’t say it’s completely stopping me from doing things. I’m still getting to work, still taking care of my family stuff, all of that.

But my mornings take longer now, and then I end up getting to school already feeling rushed, like I’m behind before the day even starts. And with teaching, that’s a bad feeling because the kids come in with all this energy and you’re supposed to be ready — and then I’m still mentally back at home thinking, “Did I leave enough time?” or whatever.

Lesson prep still gets done, but it doesn’t feel as smooth. And even my evening journaling, which used to feel calming, kind of restorative, doesn’t really feel that way lately. It’s like I’m doing it, but I’m not settling into it the same way. So… yeah, it interferes, just not in a dramatic way, I guess.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F41.1, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I came in because I keep having these thoughts that I’ve forgotten something important. Like with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll worry I left it out wrong or didn’t handle it right, even though I’m pretty sure I did. And at school, I’ll get stuck thinking maybe I didn’t set up the classroom materials safely enough.\n\nIt’s probably not even a big thing, I know that, but the doubt just keeps coming back. I’m a teacher, so I’m used to being careful—kids notice everything, and honestly organizing supplies can be its own whole project—but this feels different. It’s like my brain won’t just let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or checks to try and get rid of these doubts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, not like a whole big ritual, but before I leave the house or my classroom I’ll end up going back over the same things a few times. Like checking the pill organizer, or the stove, or my lesson plans—sometimes I’ll just stand there and replay what I did in my head.\n\nIt’s annoying because I’ll tell myself, “Okay, you already checked that,” but then I’ll think, well, what if I checked too fast? And then I’m late, which is funny because I’m always telling my students to be ready on time… but I can’t seem to get out the door until I feel calm enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge at times when you're not thinking about these specific worries?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I’m just on edge all the time, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I notice it when something specific pops into my head.\n\nThough, I mean, teaching does make you a little alert all day anyway—like if the room gets too quiet, that’s usually not good. But I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're not thinking about these specific worries about forgetting something or not doing something correctly?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really? Or at least I haven’t noticed it in that general way. I can get tense during a hectic school day, but that’s kind of normal, like when the copier jams right before class or someone spills glue everywhere.\n\nBut if you mean just sitting there feeling anxious for no reason, I don’t know… I wouldn’t say that’s a big thing for me. It’s more that something specific sets me off, and then I get stuck on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or repeating these actions helps to reduce your anxiety or unease about possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s exactly why I do it. Like, if I check the stove or the pill organizer again, or look back over my lesson plans one more time, there’s this little drop in the nervous feeling.\n\nIt’s not like I enjoy doing it. I’ll be standing there with my bag on my shoulder, keys in my hand, and then I’m thinking, “Did I actually do that, or did I just think about doing it?” And then I go back. Sometimes I don’t even touch anything, I just sort of replay it in my head until it feels… settled enough.\n\nBut then, of course, I’m running late, and then I’m annoyed with myself. My students always notice when I come in a little flustered, too, which is embarrassing. So it helps for a minute, I guess, but it also kind of causes problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, beyond just these worries about forgetting something or not doing something correctly?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s those kinds of thoughts, I think. Like the “did I forget something important” kind.\n\nIt’s not just, “Oh shoot, I forgot my lunch,” it feels more nagging than that—like I’ll suddenly think, did I leave my aunt’s medicine out wrong, or did I set up something in the classroom in a way that isn’t safe? And even if I’m pretty sure I handled it, the thought pops back in anyway.\n\nSorry, I’m thinking of my classroom because yesterday the kids were using scissors and glue sticks and one of them somehow glued a paper to his sleeve, which is not really the point. But yeah—the repeating thoughts are usually about whether I missed something important or didn’t do it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other thoughts or urges that keep coming back and feel like you need to act on them, aside from the worries about forgetting something or not doing something correctly?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there are a bunch of other ones, no. It’s mostly that same sort of… did I forget something important, did I leave something wrong.\n\nLike with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll think, “Wait, did I set that out correctly?” even when I’m pretty sure I did. Or at school, if I set up materials for the kids, I’ll suddenly wonder if I made it unsafe somehow. Which is silly sometimes because it’ll be, like, construction paper and glue sticks, but then you know, kids can turn anything into chaos.\n\nSo, not really other urges, I guess. It’s more that doubt that keeps coming back and makes me want to check or mentally go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts or behaviors are interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still doing everything I need to do. I get to work, I take care of family things, all of that. It’s not like I’m just not functioning.\n\nBut it does make things harder, especially in the mornings. I’ll think I’m ready to leave and then suddenly I’m checking something again, and then somehow ten minutes are gone. Then I get to school feeling like I’m already behind before the kids have even come in, which is not a great way to start a day.\n\nAnd lesson prep takes longer sometimes, or it feels less… smooth, I guess. I used to actually like sitting down with my journal in the evening too, just to unwind, and now even that doesn’t feel as calming as it used to. I’ll be trying to write and then my brain is still circling back to whether I did everything right. So, it’s not ruining my life, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors beyond checking things, like a specific way of doing daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Not really like… I don’t have a special way I have to tap things or count, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that before I leave the house, or before I leave my classroom, I end up going back over the same things a few times. Like I’ll check my aunt’s pill organizer, or the stove, or my lesson plans, and then sometimes I’ll just stand there silently replaying what I did in my head.\n\nAnd I know that sounds like checking, but it’s also this whole little routine of “okay, let me make sure one more time,” until I feel calm enough to actually walk away. Which is annoying because then I’m late, and then I’m annoyed at myself, and then the kids come in asking if we’re doing art today and I’m still mentally back at the stove.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts mostly about preventing something bad from happening or making a mistake, rather than other types of concerns?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly. It’s not really like random worries about… I don’t know, germs or something else. It’s more that nagging thought that I forgot something important or made a mistake that could matter.\n\nLike with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll think, “Did I leave it out wrong?” even if I know I probably handled it. Or with my classroom stuff, I’ll start wondering if I set something up in a way that isn’t safe, even when it’s just normal supplies and I’ve done it a million times.\n\nAnd then my brain just keeps bringing it back up. It’s kind of like when you leave the grocery store and you’re sure you forgot one thing, except it’s not about bananas, it’s about something that feels more serious. So yeah, I’d say it’s mostly about preventing something bad or not messing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are affecting your relationships with your students, colleagues, or family members?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really hurting my relationships exactly… like, I’m still showing up for my students, and I’m doing what I need to do with my family.\n\nBut I do notice I come in already kind of rushed sometimes, especially in the mornings. Like I’ll get to school and feel behind before the day has even started, and then I’m trying to switch into teacher mode while my brain is still catching up. Kids pick up on energy, you know? I always tell them, “Take a breath, start fresh,” which is funny because I’m not always doing that myself.\n\nWith colleagues, I don’t think anyone’s upset with me or anything. It’s more that lesson prep can take me longer, or it doesn’t feel as smooth. And at home, even my journaling — which used to be my little reset thing — doesn’t feel as restful as it used to. So, relationships… maybe not directly, but it affects the way I’m arriving to people, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel like these thoughts or behaviors are causing problems for you that we haven't talked about yet?\n\nPatient: I mean, not in a huge, life-falling-apart way. I’m still getting to work, still helping with my family stuff, still doing the things I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut mornings are probably the biggest one. They just take longer than they should, and then I get to school feeling like I’m already behind, which is the worst feeling when you’ve got a room full of kids coming in with all their stories and backpacks and someone can’t find their folder. And I’m trying to be calm, but in my head I’m like, “Okay, you were supposed to be here ten minutes earlier.”\n\nAnd lesson prep too, a little. It’s not that I can’t do it, it just doesn’t feel as smooth as it used to. Even my journaling at night — I used to really like that, I have all these half-filled notebooks, some with nice covers I bought because I thought that would make me more consistent — but lately it doesn’t feel as restful. It’s more like another thing I’m trying to settle my brain with, and it doesn’t always work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that feel like they are forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s actually why I came in. It’s like I’ll be trying to think about the day, or what I need for class, and then this thought just pops in like, “Wait, did you forget something important?”\n\nLike with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll suddenly wonder if I left it out wrong, even if I’m pretty sure I didn’t. Or I’ll be in my classroom and start thinking, “Did I set those materials up safely?” even though it’s normal stuff and I’ve done it before.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, it’s just… nagging. Like the doubt keeps coming back and interrupting whatever I was trying to focus on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts are coming into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say so. It’s not like I sit down and choose to think, “Let me worry about this again.” It just kind of barges in.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of something totally normal — planning a lesson, or honestly even making coffee, which I keep meaning to stop buying from that one place because it’s gotten so expensive — and then my brain goes, “What if you forgot something important?” Like my aunt’s medicine, or whether I set something up safely in the classroom.\n\nAnd even when I can tell myself, “No, you probably handled it,” the doubt still pops back up. So yes, it does feel against my will, I guess. Like nagging, more than anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts are unwelcome and you wish you could stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely unwelcome. I mean, I don’t want to be standing there thinking about whether I left my aunt’s medicine out wrong when I know I already dealt with it, or replaying whether the classroom materials are set up safely.\n\nIt’s like the thought pops in and I’m trying to move on, and then it’s back again. And I know that sounds small, but it’s just annoying and tiring. Like, I wish my brain would just accept, “You handled it,” and let me go on with my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts are intrusive, meaning they interrupt your other thoughts or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, they do interrupt me. That’s kind of what made me finally come in, because I’ll be doing something else and then this thought cuts in, like, “Did you forget something important?”\n\nIt’ll be about my aunt’s medicine, like maybe I left it out wrong, or at school I’ll suddenly wonder if I didn’t set up the classroom materials safely. And it’s not like I really think I did something terrible, but the doubt just keeps popping back into my head anyway.\n\nLike I’ll try to get back to what I was doing, and then—sorry, this reminds me, I even do this when I’m trying to get ready in the morning and I’m already running late, which is always when my coffee maker takes forever—but yeah, the thought comes back and kind of pulls me away from whatever I was focused on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are significantly interfering with your ability to enjoy your daily activities or perform your job as a teacher?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat, but I wouldn’t say it’s completely stopping me from doing things. I’m still getting to work, still taking care of my family stuff, all of that.\n\nBut my mornings take longer now, and then I end up getting to school already feeling rushed, like I’m behind before the day even starts. And with teaching, that’s a bad feeling because the kids come in with all this energy and you’re supposed to be ready — and then I’m still mentally back at home thinking, “Did I leave enough time?” or whatever.\n\nLesson prep still gets done, but it doesn’t feel as smooth. And even my evening journaling, which used to feel calming, kind of restorative, doesn’t really feel that way lately. It’s like I’m doing it, but I’m not settling into it the same way. So… yeah, it interferes, just not in a dramatic way, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yeah, it’s uncomfortable. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart over it, but it’s this nagging feeling that I forgot something important, and it just… sits there.

Yeah, I mean… yes, but not in a way where everything is falling apart. I’m still getting through work, and I’m still doing the family things I need to do.

It’s more like everything has this extra drag on it. My mornings take longer, and then I get to school feeling rushed, like I’m already behind before the day even starts. Which is hard because once the kids are there, there’s no slow warm-up, you know? They’re just ready.

And lesson prep still happens, but it doesn’t feel as smooth. Even my evening journaling—usually that helps me kind of reset, and lately it just doesn’t feel as calming or restorative. So overall, yeah, it affects my day-to-day. Just… mildly, but enough that I notice it.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F41.1, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I came in because I keep having these thoughts that I’ve forgotten something important. Like with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll worry I left it out wrong or didn’t handle it right, even though I’m pretty sure I did. And at school, I’ll get stuck thinking maybe I didn’t set up the classroom materials safely enough.\n\nIt’s probably not even a big thing, I know that, but the doubt just keeps coming back. I’m a teacher, so I’m used to being careful—kids notice everything, and honestly organizing supplies can be its own whole project—but this feels different. It’s like my brain won’t just let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or checks to try and get rid of these doubts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, not like a whole big ritual, but before I leave the house or my classroom I’ll end up going back over the same things a few times. Like checking the pill organizer, or the stove, or my lesson plans—sometimes I’ll just stand there and replay what I did in my head.\n\nIt’s annoying because I’ll tell myself, “Okay, you already checked that,” but then I’ll think, well, what if I checked too fast? And then I’m late, which is funny because I’m always telling my students to be ready on time… but I can’t seem to get out the door until I feel calm enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge at times when you're not thinking about these specific worries?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I’m just on edge all the time, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I notice it when something specific pops into my head.\n\nThough, I mean, teaching does make you a little alert all day anyway—like if the room gets too quiet, that’s usually not good. But I’m not sure if that’s the same thing you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're not thinking about these specific worries about forgetting something or not doing something correctly?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really? Or at least I haven’t noticed it in that general way. I can get tense during a hectic school day, but that’s kind of normal, like when the copier jams right before class or someone spills glue everywhere.\n\nBut if you mean just sitting there feeling anxious for no reason, I don’t know… I wouldn’t say that’s a big thing for me. It’s more that something specific sets me off, and then I get stuck on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking or repeating these actions helps to reduce your anxiety or unease about possibly forgetting something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s exactly why I do it. Like, if I check the stove or the pill organizer again, or look back over my lesson plans one more time, there’s this little drop in the nervous feeling.\n\nIt’s not like I enjoy doing it. I’ll be standing there with my bag on my shoulder, keys in my hand, and then I’m thinking, “Did I actually do that, or did I just think about doing it?” And then I go back. Sometimes I don’t even touch anything, I just sort of replay it in my head until it feels… settled enough.\n\nBut then, of course, I’m running late, and then I’m annoyed with myself. My students always notice when I come in a little flustered, too, which is embarrassing. So it helps for a minute, I guess, but it also kind of causes problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, beyond just these worries about forgetting something or not doing something correctly?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s those kinds of thoughts, I think. Like the “did I forget something important” kind.\n\nIt’s not just, “Oh shoot, I forgot my lunch,” it feels more nagging than that—like I’ll suddenly think, did I leave my aunt’s medicine out wrong, or did I set up something in the classroom in a way that isn’t safe? And even if I’m pretty sure I handled it, the thought pops back in anyway.\n\nSorry, I’m thinking of my classroom because yesterday the kids were using scissors and glue sticks and one of them somehow glued a paper to his sleeve, which is not really the point. But yeah—the repeating thoughts are usually about whether I missed something important or didn’t do it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other thoughts or urges that keep coming back and feel like you need to act on them, aside from the worries about forgetting something or not doing something correctly?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there are a bunch of other ones, no. It’s mostly that same sort of… did I forget something important, did I leave something wrong.\n\nLike with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll think, “Wait, did I set that out correctly?” even when I’m pretty sure I did. Or at school, if I set up materials for the kids, I’ll suddenly wonder if I made it unsafe somehow. Which is silly sometimes because it’ll be, like, construction paper and glue sticks, but then you know, kids can turn anything into chaos.\n\nSo, not really other urges, I guess. It’s more that doubt that keeps coming back and makes me want to check or mentally go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts or behaviors are interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still doing everything I need to do. I get to work, I take care of family things, all of that. It’s not like I’m just not functioning.\n\nBut it does make things harder, especially in the mornings. I’ll think I’m ready to leave and then suddenly I’m checking something again, and then somehow ten minutes are gone. Then I get to school feeling like I’m already behind before the kids have even come in, which is not a great way to start a day.\n\nAnd lesson prep takes longer sometimes, or it feels less… smooth, I guess. I used to actually like sitting down with my journal in the evening too, just to unwind, and now even that doesn’t feel as calming as it used to. I’ll be trying to write and then my brain is still circling back to whether I did everything right. So, it’s not ruining my life, but it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific rituals or behaviors beyond checking things, like a specific way of doing daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Not really like… I don’t have a special way I have to tap things or count, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more that before I leave the house, or before I leave my classroom, I end up going back over the same things a few times. Like I’ll check my aunt’s pill organizer, or the stove, or my lesson plans, and then sometimes I’ll just stand there silently replaying what I did in my head.\n\nAnd I know that sounds like checking, but it’s also this whole little routine of “okay, let me make sure one more time,” until I feel calm enough to actually walk away. Which is annoying because then I’m late, and then I’m annoyed at myself, and then the kids come in asking if we’re doing art today and I’m still mentally back at the stove.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts mostly about preventing something bad from happening or making a mistake, rather than other types of concerns?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly. It’s not really like random worries about… I don’t know, germs or something else. It’s more that nagging thought that I forgot something important or made a mistake that could matter.\n\nLike with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll think, “Did I leave it out wrong?” even if I know I probably handled it. Or with my classroom stuff, I’ll start wondering if I set something up in a way that isn’t safe, even when it’s just normal supplies and I’ve done it a million times.\n\nAnd then my brain just keeps bringing it back up. It’s kind of like when you leave the grocery store and you’re sure you forgot one thing, except it’s not about bananas, it’s about something that feels more serious. So yeah, I’d say it’s mostly about preventing something bad or not messing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are affecting your relationships with your students, colleagues, or family members?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really hurting my relationships exactly… like, I’m still showing up for my students, and I’m doing what I need to do with my family.\n\nBut I do notice I come in already kind of rushed sometimes, especially in the mornings. Like I’ll get to school and feel behind before the day has even started, and then I’m trying to switch into teacher mode while my brain is still catching up. Kids pick up on energy, you know? I always tell them, “Take a breath, start fresh,” which is funny because I’m not always doing that myself.\n\nWith colleagues, I don’t think anyone’s upset with me or anything. It’s more that lesson prep can take me longer, or it doesn’t feel as smooth. And at home, even my journaling — which used to be my little reset thing — doesn’t feel as restful as it used to. So, relationships… maybe not directly, but it affects the way I’m arriving to people, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel like these thoughts or behaviors are causing problems for you that we haven't talked about yet?\n\nPatient: I mean, not in a huge, life-falling-apart way. I’m still getting to work, still helping with my family stuff, still doing the things I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut mornings are probably the biggest one. They just take longer than they should, and then I get to school feeling like I’m already behind, which is the worst feeling when you’ve got a room full of kids coming in with all their stories and backpacks and someone can’t find their folder. And I’m trying to be calm, but in my head I’m like, “Okay, you were supposed to be here ten minutes earlier.”\n\nAnd lesson prep too, a little. It’s not that I can’t do it, it just doesn’t feel as smooth as it used to. Even my journaling at night — I used to really like that, I have all these half-filled notebooks, some with nice covers I bought because I thought that would make me more consistent — but lately it doesn’t feel as restful. It’s more like another thing I’m trying to settle my brain with, and it doesn’t always work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that feel like they are forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s actually why I came in. It’s like I’ll be trying to think about the day, or what I need for class, and then this thought just pops in like, “Wait, did you forget something important?”\n\nLike with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll suddenly wonder if I left it out wrong, even if I’m pretty sure I didn’t. Or I’ll be in my classroom and start thinking, “Did I set those materials up safely?” even though it’s normal stuff and I’ve done it before.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, it’s just… nagging. Like the doubt keeps coming back and interrupting whatever I was trying to focus on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts are coming into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say so. It’s not like I sit down and choose to think, “Let me worry about this again.” It just kind of barges in.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of something totally normal — planning a lesson, or honestly even making coffee, which I keep meaning to stop buying from that one place because it’s gotten so expensive — and then my brain goes, “What if you forgot something important?” Like my aunt’s medicine, or whether I set something up safely in the classroom.\n\nAnd even when I can tell myself, “No, you probably handled it,” the doubt still pops back up. So yes, it does feel against my will, I guess. Like nagging, more than anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts are unwelcome and you wish you could stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely unwelcome. I mean, I don’t want to be standing there thinking about whether I left my aunt’s medicine out wrong when I know I already dealt with it, or replaying whether the classroom materials are set up safely.\n\nIt’s like the thought pops in and I’m trying to move on, and then it’s back again. And I know that sounds small, but it’s just annoying and tiring. Like, I wish my brain would just accept, “You handled it,” and let me go on with my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts are intrusive, meaning they interrupt your other thoughts or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, they do interrupt me. That’s kind of what made me finally come in, because I’ll be doing something else and then this thought cuts in, like, “Did you forget something important?”\n\nIt’ll be about my aunt’s medicine, like maybe I left it out wrong, or at school I’ll suddenly wonder if I didn’t set up the classroom materials safely. And it’s not like I really think I did something terrible, but the doubt just keeps popping back into my head anyway.\n\nLike I’ll try to get back to what I was doing, and then—sorry, this reminds me, I even do this when I’m trying to get ready in the morning and I’m already running late, which is always when my coffee maker takes forever—but yeah, the thought comes back and kind of pulls me away from whatever I was focused on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are significantly interfering with your ability to enjoy your daily activities or perform your job as a teacher?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat, but I wouldn’t say it’s completely stopping me from doing things. I’m still getting to work, still taking care of my family stuff, all of that.\n\nBut my mornings take longer now, and then I end up getting to school already feeling rushed, like I’m behind before the day even starts. And with teaching, that’s a bad feeling because the kids come in with all this energy and you’re supposed to be ready — and then I’m still mentally back at home thinking, “Did I leave enough time?” or whatever.\n\nLesson prep still gets done, but it doesn’t feel as smooth. And even my evening journaling, which used to feel calming, kind of restorative, doesn’t really feel that way lately. It’s like I’m doing it, but I’m not settling into it the same way. So… yeah, it interferes, just not in a dramatic way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and behaviors are causing you a lot of distress or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s uncomfortable. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m falling apart over it, but it’s this nagging feeling that I forgot something important, and it just… sits there.\n\nLike with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll think, “Did I leave it out wrong?” even when I’m pretty sure I handled it. Or at school, I’ll start worrying about whether I set up the classroom materials safely. And then I’ll try to tell myself, “No, you probably did it right,” but the doubt keeps popping back in.\n\nIt’s frustrating more than anything. Kind of like when you have a song stuck in your head, except instead of a song it’s this little alarm bell going off. So yeah, it causes distress, but mild-ish, I guess. Still enough that I came in, because I’m tired of it.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and behaviors significantly affecting your overall well-being or daily functioning?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… yes, but not in a way where everything is falling apart. I’m still getting through work, and I’m still doing the family things I need to do.\n\nIt’s more like everything has this extra drag on it. My mornings take longer, and then I get to school feeling rushed, like I’m already behind before the day even starts. Which is hard because once the kids are there, there’s no slow warm-up, you know? They’re just ready.\n\nAnd lesson prep still happens, but it doesn’t feel as smooth. Even my evening journaling—usually that helps me kind of reset, and lately it just doesn’t feel as calming or restorative. So overall, yeah, it affects my day-to-day. Just… mildly, but enough that I notice it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

A little, yeah. I mean, I’m still doing my job—I’m not missing school or anything like that, and I still get the lesson prep done. But it feels like I’m starting the day with less room, if that makes sense.

I’m still getting to work, still helping with my family stuff, still doing the things I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut mornings are probably the biggest one. I mean, I don’t want to be standing there thinking about whether I left my aunt’s medicine out wrong when I know I already dealt with it, or replaying whether the classroom materials are set up safely.\n\nIt’s like the thought pops in and I’m trying to move on, and then it’s back again. And I know that sounds small, but it’s just annoying and tiring. They’re just ready.\n\nAnd lesson prep still happens, but it doesn’t feel as smooth.
